Overall Infection Resistance
Observation window for resistance metrics: 2022-2025 calibration window
Combinations included: 1233
Note: MDR-TB and rifampicin combinations are excluded from these fit metrics because all TB cases are modelled as rifampicin-resistant by definition, which would skew overall resistance metrics. Listeria monocytogenes is also excluded because its very low infection incidence yields unstable resistance percentages at simulation scale.
These fit metrics average over bacteria/drug combinations, whereas the microbiome benchmarks below describe the share of the total population carrying any resistant microbiome.

---
Footnotes

(1) Infection deaths target: 6.4 million model-scope bacterial infection deaths per year.
    The target is aligned to a person-level simulation numerator: sepsis-related plus
    non-sepsis infection deaths with at least one contributing infection other than
    H. pylori or MDR-TB. Concurrent excluded infections do not remove an otherwise
    in-scope death. GBD 2019 estimated 13.7 million
    total infection-related deaths globally, including viral, parasitic, and fungal
    causes. The 33-pathogen bacterial analysis estimated approximately 7.7 million
    bacterial-pathogen-associated deaths, while Murray et al. (2022, Lancet 399:629-655,
    GRAM study) estimated 4.95 million deaths associated with bacterial AMR specifically.
    The per-organism mortality targets encoded for this model sum to about 7.39 million
    including H. pylori gastric-cancer mortality and MDR-TB. Excluding those two
    out-of-scope categories gives 6.40 million, used here as the like-for-like headline
    calibration target.

(2) Antibiotic use target: 100 million people on antibiotics on an average day.
    This target was revised downward because Klein et al. (2018, PNAS 115:E3463-E3470)
    is fundamentally a consumption paper reporting DDDs from sales data, not a direct
    count of unique people on treatment on an average day. Treating DDD totals as daily
    users tends to overstate person-prevalence because prescribed daily doses vary by
    drug and syndrome, some regimens exceed 1 DDD/day, and sales volumes include leakage
    from wastage, stock buffering, and non-human channels. WHO AWaRe monitoring gives a
    global central tendency around 14.5 DDDs per 1,000 inhabitants per day, which would
    imply roughly 119 million DDD-equivalents/day at a world population of 8.2 billion,
    but unique daily users should sit below that DDD total in a person-based model. The
    revised 100 million target therefore treats antibiotic prevalence as a pragmatic
    person-day calibration anchor rather than a literal transcription of the Klein DDD
    estimate.

(3) Bacterial infection incidence target: 15% of the world population per year.
    GBD 2019 estimated approximately 11 billion incident episodes of infectious
    disease globally per year across all causes (Vos et al. 2020, Lancet 396:1204-1222).
    The bacterial fraction is roughly 40-50% of total infectious episodes, implying
    ~1.0-1.2 billion bacterial infection episodes per year, or 12-15% of the 8.2
    billion world population. Estimates vary: Laxminarayan et al. (2016, Lancet Infect
    Dis 16:e51-e71) cited bacterial infection incidence consistent with 10-15% global
    prevalence. Excler et al. (2023, Vaccine) and various national surveillance studies
    (ECDC, CDC) report community-acquired bacterial infection rates of 10-20% per
    year in HIC populations, with higher rates in LMIC settings. The 15% target
    represents a mid-range estimate for global annual bacterial infection incidence.

(4) Sepsis target: 30 million incident cases per year (bacterial sepsis only).
    This target was revised downward because Rudd et al. (2020, Lancet 395:200-211)
    estimated 48.9 million all-cause sepsis cases globally, whereas this model only
    simulates bacterial infections. The previous 35 million target forced the model
    too close to the all-cause literature for a bacteria-only system and left too little
    room for viral, fungal, and parasitic sepsis outside model scope. A 30 million
    target keeps the implied bacterial share near 60% of the Rudd total, which is still
    substantial, but is a more defensible central benchmark for a bacteria-only model.

(5) Per-bacteria infection incidence targets sourced primarily from: Antimicrobial
    Resistance Collaborators (2022, Lancet 399:629-655, GRAM study) for major
    pathogens (E. coli, S. aureus, S. pneumoniae, K. pneumoniae, P. aeruginosa,
    A. baumannii, E. faecium, E. faecalis); WHO 2024 STI estimates for N.
    gonorrhoeae (~82M/yr), C. trachomatis (~129M/yr), T. pallidum (~8M/yr);
    WHO FERG (Havelaar et al. 2015, PLoS Med 12:e1001923) for foodborne
    pathogens (Shigella ~188M, Campylobacter ~96M, non-typhoidal Salmonella);
    WHO Global TB Report 2024 for MDR-TB (~400-500K/yr). Smaller healthcare-
    associated pathogens (Citrobacter, Serratia, Stenotrophomonas, Morganella,
    Providencia, B. cepacia) use placeholder estimates extrapolated from regional
    nosocomial surveillance data with greater uncertainty.

(6) Per-bacteria carriage targets sourced from: Human Microbiome Project (HMP,
    NIH 2012) for core gut commensals (E. coli ~95%, B. fragilis ~85%,
    E. faecalis ~80%, S. epidermidis ~95%); Wertheim et al. (2005, Lancet Infect
    Dis 5:751-762) for S. aureus nasal carriage (~20-30%); Bogaert et al. (2004,
    Lancet Infect Dis 4:144-154) for S. pneumoniae nasopharyngeal carriage
    (~35% population-weighted average); CDC GBS screening guidelines for S.
    agalactiae (~25% vaginal/rectal colonization). K. pneumoniae gut carriage
    (20%) from Gorrie et al. (2017, PNAS 114:7655-7660) and regional point-
    prevalence surveys. H. pylori and L. pneumophila set to zero carriage by
    model design (infection-only pathway, no microbiome colonization).

(7) Per-bacteria death targets sourced primarily from: Antimicrobial Resistance
    Collaborators (2022, Lancet 399:629-655, GRAM study) for major pathogens
    including S. aureus (1.1M associated deaths), E. coli (0.83M), K. pneumoniae
    (0.71M), S. pneumoniae (0.65M), A. baumannii (0.38M), and P. aeruginosa
    (0.33M); IARC/GBD for H. pylori gastric cancer deaths (0.8M); WHO Global
    TB Report 2024 for MDR-TB (0.19M); WHO estimates for typhoid (0.14M),
    cholera (0.1M), and pertussis (0.16M); GBD 2019 for diarrhoeal deaths
    (Shigella 0.2M). Smaller healthcare-associated organisms use placeholder
    estimates extrapolated from case-fatality rates applied to incidence data.

(8) Drug class share targets derived from multiple surveillance sources. ECDC
    Simulation shares use total active antibiotic drug-days across the configured
    drug classes as the denominator, so simulation class shares are compositional
    and sum to 100% apart from rounding.
    ESAC-Net (European Surveillance of Antimicrobial Consumption, annual reports,
    esac-net.europa.eu) provides class-level DDD/1000/day breakdowns for 30 EU/EEA
    countries. Klein et al. (2018, PNAS 115:E3463-E3470) provided global class-level
    consumption from IQVIA pharmaceutical sales data across 76 countries. WHO AWaRe
    classification reports (2019-2023) confirm Access-group antibiotics (penicillins,
    beta-lactam combinations, older cephalosporins, sulfonamides, nitrofurans,
    tetracyclines) account for ~60% of global consumption, Watch-group drugs
    (fluoroquinolones, macrolides, 3G cephalosporins) ~30%, and Reserve drugs <1%.
    Van Boeckel et al. (2014, Lancet Infect Dis 14:742-750) provided additional
    global class breakdowns. Historical targets (2000, 1975, 1950) are interpolated
    from class introduction dates and early adoption curves.

(9) Microbiome resistance simulation output (not calibrated). The simulation
    tracks whether each individual carries ANY resistant mechanism in their
    microbiome for each bacteria/drug combination. No reliable external targets
    exist for this "any resistance" definition: surveillance data measures
    resistance of cultured clinical isolates (dominant strain), while the
    simulation counts any mechanism present. Forslund et al. (2013, Nature
    Commun 4:2151) found resistance genes in virtually all human gut
    metagenomes, suggesting the true "any resistance" proportion is 80-100%.
    These values are presented for information only and are excluded from the
    calibration score.

(10) Per-bacteria/drug resistance prevalence targets sourced from WHO GLASS
     (Global Antimicrobial Resistance and Use Surveillance System, 2022 report)
     and Antimicrobial Resistance Collaborators (2022, Lancet 399:629-655, GRAM
     study) global median resistance proportions. Drug-specific highlights:
     carbapenem-resistant A. baumannii (CRAB) >50% (WHO Critical Priority);
     ESBL-producing E. coli 15-25% 3GC resistance; CRKP 10-15%; MRSA 25-40%
     (GLASS/CDC 2019); fluoroquinolone-resistant Campylobacter >50% (agricultural
     use); ceftriaxone/azithromycin-resistant N. gonorrhoeae emerging (WHO 2024).
     MDR-TB estimates from WHO Global TB Report 2024. Values represent global
     medians and mask substantial regional variation (e.g. MRSA <5% in
     Scandinavia vs >50% in parts of Asia). Entries marked '.' indicate
     intrinsically resistant or inapplicable bacteria/drug combinations.

(11) Some pair-specific resistance targets may not be exactly reproducible under
     the current model structure, especially where data suggest differences between
     closely related drugs that share the same modeled mechanism pathways. These
     residual mismatches are accepted unless sustained evidence supports adding a
     corresponding mechanistic distinction to the model.

(12) Mechanism applicability is no longer duplicated in this summary table. The
     authoritative bacteria/drug/mechanism logic is defined in src/rules/mod.rs
     via mechanism_applies_to_drug(...).
